From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Malaysian citizens of Chinese ethnicity

This article is about people of Chinese ethnicity or nationality in Malaysia. For the variety of Mandarin Chinese spoken in Malaysia, see [Malaysian Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Mandarin "Malaysian Mandarin"). For Malaysians in China, see [Malaysian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_diaspora "Malaysian diaspora").

Ethnic group

| Languages |
| **Predominantly** [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Mandarin "Malaysian Mandarin") (lingua franca) **Minority** [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language "Malay language") and [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_English "Malaysian English") as medium of communication in schools and government _Mother Tongue languages_: [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien "Hokkien"), [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese "Cantonese"), [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_Chinese "Hakka Chinese"), [Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_dialect "Teochew dialect"), [Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_dialect "Fuzhou dialect"), [Hainanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainanese "Hainanese"), [Taishanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taishanese "Taishanese") and [Henghua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puxian_dialect "Puxian dialect"); [Manglish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manglish "Manglish") (creole) |
| Religion |
| **Predominantly** [![Image 12](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Dharma_Wheel_Rotating.svg/20px-Dharma_Wheel_Rotating.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dharma_Wheel_Rotating.svg)[Mahāyāna Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81y%C4%81na_Buddhism "Mahāyāna Buddhism") • [![Image 13](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Yin_yang.svg/20px-Yin_yang.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Yin_yang.svg)[Taoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism_in_Malaysia "Taoism in Malaysia") ([Chinese folk religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religion "Chinese folk religion")) **Significant minority** [![Image 14](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Christian_cross.svg/20px-Christian_cross.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Christian_cross.svg)[Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Malaysia "Christianity in Malaysia") **Minority** [![Image 15](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/IslamSymbol1.svg/20px-IslamSymbol1.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:IslamSymbol1.svg)[Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Malaysia "Islam in Malaysia") • [Non-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion "Irreligion") • other religions |

|  |

**Malaysian Chinese** or **Chinese Malaysians** are [Malaysian citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysians "Malaysians") of [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_people "Chinese people")[ethnicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicity "Ethnicity"). They form the second-largest ethnic group in Malaysia, after the [Malay-majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Malays "Malaysian Malays"), and as of 2020[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit), constituted 23.2% of the country's citizens or close to 7 million.[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-2020_census-2) In addition, Malaysian Chinese make up the second-largest community of [overseas Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Chinese "Overseas Chinese") in the world, following the [Thai Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_Chinese "Thai Chinese") in neighbouring [Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thailand "Thailand").[[a]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-3) Prior to [1963](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Agreement "Malaysia Agreement"), the ethnic Chinese community were often referred to as the **Malayan Chinese**.[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHara20039-4)[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoon1983290%E2%80%93311-5) As with most [Chinese communities in Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamboo_network "Bamboo network"), they have maintained a significant and substantial presence in the [economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Malaysia "Economy of Malaysia") of the modern-day country for at least two centuries since the era of [British Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Malaya "British Malaya").

Most Malaysian Chinese are descendants of [Southern Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_China "Southern China") immigrants who arrived in [Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia "Malaysia") between the early 19th and the mid-20th centuries before the country attained independence from British colonial rule. The majority originate from [Fujian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujian "Fujian") or the [Lingnan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingnan "Lingnan") region (modern-day [Guangdong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangdong "Guangdong"), [Guangxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangxi "Guangxi") and [Hainan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainan "Hainan")). They belong to diverse linguistic subgroups speaking Chinese such as the [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoklo_people "Hoklo people") and [Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_people "Fuzhou people") from Fujian, the [Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_people "Teochew people"), [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese_people "Cantonese people"), [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_people "Hakka people") from Guangdong, the [Hainanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainan_people "Hainan people") from Hainan and [Kwongsai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwongsai_people "Kwongsai people") from Guangxi. An earlier wave of Chinese migrants, arriving between the 13th and 17th centuries, [assimilated significant aspects of indigenous Malay culture and customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayisation "Malayisation") to forge distinct identities. These groups include the [Peranakans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peranakans "Peranakans") (or Baba–Nyonya) of [Kelantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelantan "Kelantan"), [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca "Malacca"), [Penang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penang "Penang") and [Terengganu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terengganu "Terengganu") and the [Sino-Natives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Native "Sino-Native") of [Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah "Sabah"). Characterised by a high degree of intermarriage with native populations, these communities has preserved unique culture and traditions that distinguish them from the broader Malaysian Chinese population.[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChee-Beng1988297%E2%80%93314-6)[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2005122%E2%80%93124-7)

Most Malaysian Chinese have maintained their Chinese heritage, identity, culture and language. The Malay-majority government do not classify them as part of the _[bumiputera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bumiputera\_(Malaysia) "Bumiputera (Malaysia)")_, a [preferential status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_153_of_the_Constitution_of_Malaysia "Article 153 of the Constitution of Malaysia") in the country.[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHefner200117-8) Various socioeconomic factors, including divergent fertility rates, migration trends and shifting policy dynamics, have led to a gradual decline in the Chinese population's relative share since 1957.

## Names

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: Names")]

The Malaysian Chinese are referred to as simply "Chinese" in Malaysian English, _Orang Cina_ in [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language "Malay language"), _Sina_ or _Kina_ among indigenous groups in [Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_North_Borneo_languages "Greater North Borneo languages"), _Cīṉar_ (சீனர்) in [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language "Tamil language"), _[Huárén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huaxia "Huaxia")_ (华人; 華人; 'Chinese people') or _Huáqiáo_ (华侨; 華僑; 'overseas Chinese') or _Huáyì_ (华裔; 華裔; 'ethnic Chinese') in [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese "Mandarin Chinese"), _Tn̂g-lâng_ (唐侬; 唐儂; 'Tang-dynasty people') in [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien "Hokkien"), and _[Wàhyàhn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas\_Chinese "Overseas Chinese")_ (华人; 華人; 'Chinese people') in [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese "Cantonese").

## History

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: History")]

See also: [History of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Malaysia "History of Malaysia")

### Early history

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Early history")]

[![Image 17: A boat with a dark sail](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Chinese_junk_in_Kinabatangan%2C_Borneo.JPG/250px-Chinese_junk_in_Kinabatangan%2C_Borneo.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Chinese_junk_in_Kinabatangan,_Borneo.JPG)

A Chinese [junk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junk_(ship) "Junk (ship)") in the [Kinabatangan District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinabatangan_District "Kinabatangan District") of northern Borneo, photographed by [Martin Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_and_Osa_Johnson "Martin and Osa Johnson")c.1935

[China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_China "History of China") and other early kingdoms in the [Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Archipelago "Malay Archipelago"), such as the northern area of the [Malay Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Peninsula "Malay Peninsula") and Po-Ni in western [Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo "Borneo"), have long been connected.[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-9) The first recorded movement of people from [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_dynasty "Yuan dynasty") into present-day Malaysia was the 1292 arrival of [Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire "Mongol Empire") expeditionary forces under [Kublai Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kublai_Khan "Kublai Khan") in Borneo in 1292 in preparation for their [invasion of Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Java "Mongol invasion of Java") the following year.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoyal_Society_(Great_Britain)1924157-10)[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTERunciman201111-11) Many of Khan's Chinese followers settled on the island with Chinese traders after the campaign, establishing an enclave along the [Kinabatangan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinabatangan_River "Kinabatangan River"). Their arrival was welcomed by the [indigenous people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_people "Indigenous people"), who benefited from the jars, beads, silk, and pottery brought to the island by the Chinese.[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong19982-12)[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuillotLombardPtak1998162-13)[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTERunciman201114-14)

[![Image 18: See caption](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Map_of_Ming_Chinese_empire_1415.jpg/250px-Map_of_Ming_Chinese_empire_1415.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Map_of_Ming_Chinese_empire_1415.jpg)

Map of the [Ming dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_dynasty "Ming dynasty") under the [Yongle Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yongle_Emperor "Yongle Emperor") in 1415

Chinese explorer and sailor [Zheng He](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zheng_He "Zheng He") commanded [several expeditions to south-east Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_treasure_voyages "Ming treasure voyages") between 1405 and 1430; during his third voyage, Zheng also visited [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca_Sultanate "Malacca Sultanate").[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrezina201658-15) His companion and translator, [Ma Huan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Huan "Ma Huan"), described Malacca in his _[Yingya Shenglan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yingya\_Shenglan "Yingya Shenglan")._ Formerly part of Thailand, Malacca was founded after convoys from the Ming Dynasty developed a city and the area's chief was crowned king.[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-16) During his fourth imperial-fleet visit, Ma wrote that the local king had just [converted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conversion "Religious conversion") to Islam and dressed like an [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_people "Arab people").[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaHuanFeng1970109-17)[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2007133-18) The last edition of _[Mingshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mingshi "Mingshi")_, one of the official Chinese _[Twenty-Four Histories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-Four\_Histories "Twenty-Four Histories"),_ mentioned a pre-established Chinese settlement in the area.[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan198829-19) Mentions in other records exist, especially after trade contacts were established with the [Nanyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanyang_(region) "Nanyang (region)") region.[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPurcell1947115%E2%80%93125-20) According to _Hai Yü_ (_Words about the Sea)_, written by Huang Zhong and published in 1537, the lifestyle of the Chinese community in Malacca differed from that of the local [Malays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malays_(ethnic_group) "Malays (ethnic group)").[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGroeneveldt18767-21)

This close relationship was maintained during the Islamisation of the Malacca and [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Empire "Bruneian Empire") kingdoms, whose [thalassocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thalassocracy "Thalassocracy") once covered much of present-day Malaysia. Both the Muslim sultanates pledged protection to the Chinese dynasties from further conquest by the neighbouring Javanese [Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit "Majapahit") or the Siamese [Ayutthaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom "Ayutthaya Kingdom").[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2000175-22)[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaCartier200379-23)[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Vienne201542%E2%80%9343-24) This relationship resulted in [interethnic marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interethnic_marriage "Interethnic marriage") between the sultanate's royal family and the Chinese envoy and representatives.[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaCartier200390-25)[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee201011-26)[[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOoi201550-27) Zheng He's arrival encouraged the [spread of Islam in the Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Southeast_Asia "Islam in Southeast Asia") and aided the growth of the [Chinese Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_people "Hui people") population from the Eastern Chinese coastal towns of [Fujian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujian "Fujian") and [Canton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangzhou "Guangzhou"), with many of their traders arriving in the coastal towns of present-day Malaysia and [Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia "Indonesia") by the early 15th century.[[27]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004115-28)[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMa201711-29) In addition to the early settlements in Kinabatangan and Malacca, two more old Chinese settlements are located in [Terengganu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terengganu "Terengganu") and the [Penang Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penang_Island "Penang Island") as part of trade networks with their respective areas.[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee1965306%E2%80%93314-30)[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong20169%E2%80%9321-31)[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWadeChin2018249-32)

### Colonial era (1500–1900)

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Colonial era (1500–1900)")]

[![Image 19: Cemetery on a hill with trees](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Bukit_Chena%2C_Malacca_%287886253620%29.jpg/250px-Bukit_Chena%2C_Malacca_%287886253620%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bukit_Chena,_Malacca_(7886253620).jpg)

1860–1900 photograph of [Bukit Cina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Cina "Bukit Cina") (Chinese Hill) in [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca_Sultanate "Malacca Sultanate"), one of Malaysia's oldest Chinese cemeteries[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong20169%E2%80%9321-31)

Although many Chinese traders avoided [Portuguese Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Malacca "Portuguese Malacca") after its [1511 conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Malacca_(1511) "Capture of Malacca (1511)"), the flow of emigrants from China continued.[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuillotLombardPtak1998179-33) The [Zhengde Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhengde_Emperor "Zhengde Emperor") retaliated against the Portuguese for their activities in Malacca during the [Ming dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_dynasty "Ming dynasty") after the arrival of their fleet in Canton.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang193467-34) The Chinese emperor was reluctant to help the deposed Malaccan ruler reclaim his position, however, since the dynasty's foreign policy was changing to maintain friendly relations with the Portuguese.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang193467-34)[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWadeChin2018144-35)

His successor, the [Jiajing Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiajing_Emperor "Jiajing Emperor"), changed the attitude of the Ming court by executing two government officials and reaffirming the importance of Malaccan issues in major policy decisions.[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWadeChin2018144-35) Some Chinese, including those from Fujian (defying Ming-Dynasty regulation for the sake of trade), informed the Portuguese of the trade route between [Guangdong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangdong "Guangdong") and Siam. Since the local Malaccan Chinese were not treated favourably by the Portuguese, they and most overseas Chinese refused to cooperate with them.[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-36) Through the Portuguese administration in Malacca, Chinese Muslims sided with other Islamic traders against the latter by providing ships and [human capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital "Human capital").[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorschberg200412-37) Negotiations were later held in Guangdong between Chinese officials and Portuguese envoys about Malaccan issues, with Malacca remaining under Portuguese control.[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWadeChin2018145%E2%80%93146-38)

[![Image 20: Formal outdoor group photograph](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Malaysia%3B_Captain_China_surrounded_by_his_Chinese_and_Malay_Wellcome_V0037516.jpg/250px-Malaysia%3B_Captain_China_surrounded_by_his_Chinese_and_Malay_Wellcome_V0037516.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Malaysia;_Captain_China_surrounded_by_his_Chinese_and_Malay_Wellcome_V0037516.jpg)

The [Hai San Secret Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hai_San_Secret_Society "Hai San Secret Society")'s "[Captain China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chung_Keng_Quee "Chung Keng Quee")" with his followers in [Selangor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selangor_Sultanate "Selangor Sultanate"), June 1874

After the 1641 [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Empire "Dutch Empire")[takeover of Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Malacca_(1641) "Battle of Malacca (1641)"),[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBluss%C3%A9200923-39) many local Malaccan Chinese were hired to construct Dutch buildings.[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELimFernando2006126-40) The Dutch found the Chinese industrious, and encouraged their participation in the colony's economic life;[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamanathan199467-41) the Dutch also established a settlement in [Perak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perak_Sultanate "Perak Sultanate") in 1650 through an earlier treaty with [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh_Sultanate "Aceh Sultanate")[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClifford1911479-42) and suggested that Alauddin, the 17th Sultan of Perak, allow the Chinese to develop [tin mines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin_mining "Tin mining"). This facilitated Alauddin's plan to request more Chinese workers from Malacca, and the sultan promised to punish any official guilty of mistreating the Chinese.[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhooLubis20055-43)[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMizushimaSouzaFlynn2014210-44)

With the sultan's consent, the Chinese played a leading role in the tin-mining industry. The [Fourth Anglo-Dutch War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Anglo-Dutch_War "Fourth Anglo-Dutch War") in 1780 adversely affected the tin trade, however, and many Chinese miners left.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhoo197239-45) Early Chinese settlements in Malacca and several areas along the archipelago's coasts notwithstanding, most mass Chinese migration to Malaysia occurred after the founding of British settlements in [Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Malaya "British Malaya") and [Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Borneo "British Borneo") during the early 19th century.[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirschman1986338-46)

[![Image 21: A large group of people, some sitting and others standing](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/British_officials_in_Perak_with_wealthy_Chinese_person.jpg/250px-British_officials_in_Perak_with_wealthy_Chinese_person.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:British_officials_in_Perak_with_wealthy_Chinese_person.jpg)

Chinese [tycoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tycoon "Tycoon") Ng Boo Bee with British officials in [Perak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perak_Sultanate "Perak Sultanate") in 1904

A 1786 British settlement in Penang and another in [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_years_of_modern_Singapore "Founding years of modern Singapore") in 1819 triggered a mass emigration from China to the Malay Peninsula.[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirschman1986338-46) After the establishment of British rule in [Labuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_Labuan "Crown Colony of Labuan") in 1846, more ethnic Chinese (primarily [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_people "Hakka people"), and many from Singapore) arrived in [British Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Borneo "British Borneo"). The migration continued through the first few years of the [North Borneo Chartered Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo_Chartered_Company "North Borneo Chartered Company").[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong1999132-47) Chinese migration to Labuan and North Borneo was largely confined to the [agricultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural "Agricultural") sector; migration to the [Raj of Sarawak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raj_of_Sarawak "Raj of Sarawak") was largely restricted to the mining and agricultural sectors.[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHewitt186431-48)[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiew19692-49) Rajah [Charles Brooke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Brooke,_Rajah_of_Sarawak "Charles Brooke, Rajah of Sarawak") of Sarawak promoted the migration of Chinese and [Dayak people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayak_people "Dayak people") to interior [Sarawak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarawak "Sarawak") to develop the region. This resulted in the establishment of an administrative center and [bazaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bazaar "Bazaar") by the 19th century, primarily in Sarawak's [First, Second, and Third Divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divisions_of_Malaysia#Sarawak "Divisions of Malaysia").[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabble200086-50)

Rajah Charles invited Chinese [black-pepper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_pepper "Black pepper") growers from Singapore to settle in [Kuching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuching "Kuching") in 1876, and they were later joined by local Chinese miners and others from neighbouring [Dutch Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalimantan "Kalimantan").[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGurney1986268-51)[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBulbeckReidTanYiqi199868-52) With the introduction of pepper to the kingdom, pepper cultivation in Sarawak's First Division Kuching-Serian region was dominated by the Hakka; [Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_people "Fuzhou people") and [Cantonese people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese_people "Cantonese people") dominated cultivation in the Third Division Sarikei-Binatang region, making Sarawak the world's second-largest pepper producer.[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBulbeckReidTanYiqi199868-52)[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaur201648-53) After the British gained control of the four states of Perak, [Selangor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selangor_Sultanate "Selangor Sultanate"), [Negeri Sembilan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamtuan_Besar "Yamtuan Besar") and [Pahang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_of_Pahang "Sultan of Pahang"), nearly two million Chinese immigrated to Perak and Selangor.[[53]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoranDixon1991111-54)[[54]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKratoska2001b209-55)

[![Image 22: Old photo of four children in a rickshaw pulled by a man](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/The_National_geographic_magazine_%28Page_164%29_BHL40563162_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-The_National_geographic_magazine_%28Page_164%29_BHL40563162_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_National_geographic_magazine_(Page_164)_BHL40563162_(cropped).jpg)

Children in a [rickshaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulled_rickshaw "Pulled rickshaw") pulled by a Chinese [coolie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coolie "Coolie") in [Kuching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuching "Kuching") around 1919

After the discovery of [tin deposits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin_sources_and_trade_in_ancient_times "Tin sources and trade in ancient times") in [British Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Malaya "British Malaya"), many Chinese immigrants worked in the tin-mining industry.[[55]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYat196957-56) As tin mines opened in Perak, many Chinese in neighbouring Penang became wealthy.[[56]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDouwHuangIp2013165-57) Many Chinese tin-mining communities were established in the Malay Peninsula by the 1870s, particularly in [Ipoh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipoh "Ipoh") ([Kinta Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinta_Valley "Kinta Valley")), [Taiping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiping,_Perak "Taiping, Perak"), [Seremban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seremban "Seremban") and [Kuala Lumpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuala_Lumpur "Kuala Lumpur") ([Klang Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klang_Valley "Klang Valley")).[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETin_Industry_(Research_and_Development)_Board19559-58)[[58]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELinHill1979145-59) Each of the mining communities was governed by a [Kapitan Cina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapitan_Cina "Kapitan Cina"), with duties and privileges similar to those in [Johor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor_Sultanate "Johor Sultanate")'s [Kangchu system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangchu_system "Kangchu system").[[59]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELanda201658-60) Chinese migration sprang from poverty in rural China and employment opportunities in the British colonies or [protectorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorate "Protectorate"), and an estimated five million Chinese had immigrated by the 19th century.[[60]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChingMcKenna19902-61) Despite economic prosperity after immigration, the new Chinese communities split into a number of [secret societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_societies "Secret societies"). This exacerbated political unrest among the Malay aristocracy, which enlisted help from the secret societies.[[59]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELanda201658-60)[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEComber1956146%E2%80%93162-62) Contemporary local Malay politics was characterised as anarchy and civil war between people from similar backgrounds, prompting alliances between senior Malay political leaders and officials from China and Europe who were protecting their investments.[[62]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeletz1996102-63)

### Divided nationalism and turbulence (1900–1945)

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: Divided nationalism and turbulence (1900–1945)")]

See also: [Chinese revolutionary activities in Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_revolutionary_activities_in_Malaya "Chinese revolutionary activities in Malaya")

> You can help shoulder the responsibility of saving our country by donating your money, while our comrades in our country are sacrificing their lives.

—Sun Yat-sen at the [1910 Penang conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1910_Penang_conference "1910 Penang conference") in the [Straits Settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straits_Settlements "Straits Settlements") of Penang[[63]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhoo200855-64)

[![Image 23: Outdoor photo of nine men, five seated and four standing](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Fundraising_in_Ipoh_for_the_Second_Guangzhou_uprising.jpg/250px-Fundraising_in_Ipoh_for_the_Second_Guangzhou_uprising.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Fundraising_in_Ipoh_for_the_Second_Guangzhou_uprising.jpg)

[Tongmenghui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tongmenghui "Tongmenghui") fundraising meeting in [Ipoh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipoh "Ipoh") for the [Second Guangzhou Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Guangzhou_Uprising "Second Guangzhou Uprising"), c.1911

Civil wars and other conflicts among the indigenous ethnic groups ended when the British gained control of Malaya and the northern island of [Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo "Borneo") by the 1900s. However, none of the Malay kingdoms fell under colonial rule. Their related entities were politically and economically stable. Economic prosperity was fueled by British [capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_(economics) "Capital (economics)") and Chinese and Indian work forces who expanded and supplied tin and [rubber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_rubber "Natural rubber") production.[[60]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChingMcKenna19902-61)[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhoo2010-65) Before the 1911 [Xinhai Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinhai_Revolution "Xinhai Revolution") which overthrew the [Qing dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty "Qing dynasty") in China, [Tongmenghui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tongmenghui "Tongmenghui") leader [Sun Yat-sen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_Yat-sen "Sun Yat-sen") raised funds and organisational support from overseas Chinese communities (primarily in [French Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Indochina "French Indochina"), the [Dutch East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_Indies "Dutch East Indies"), [the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insular_Government_of_the_Philippine_Islands "Insular Government of the Philippine Islands"), [Siam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rattanakosin_Kingdom_(1782%E2%80%931932) "Rattanakosin Kingdom (1782–1932)"), British Singapore, Malaya and Borneo) and began [Nanyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanyang_(region) "Nanyang (region)") organising activities in [Saigon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saigon "Saigon"), [Hanoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanoi "Hanoi"), [Pontianak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontianak,_Indonesia "Pontianak, Indonesia"), [Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila "Manila"), [Bangkok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangkok "Bangkok"), Singapore and [George Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Town,_Penang "George Town, Penang").[[65]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-66) Competition arose among three groups, with each group targeting overseas Chinese (particularly in Malaya and Singapore) for support.[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhoo200824-67) One group was a pro-Qing elite who targeted wealthy Chinese; the other two were reformists and revolutionaries who advocated [constitutional reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_amendment "Constitutional amendment"), the introduction of a [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system "Parliamentary system"), and the overthrow of Qing and Manchu influence on a modern Chinese nation.[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhoo200824-67)

[![Image 24: Three young, smiling Chinese women drying dishes](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Three_Chinese_girls_working_at_the_British_mobile_canteen_in_Malaya.JPG/250px-Three_Chinese_girls_working_at_the_British_mobile_canteen_in_Malaya.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Three_Chinese_girls_working_at_the_British_mobile_canteen_in_Malaya.JPG)

Chinese women working in a British [mobile canteen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_canteen "Mobile canteen") during the [Malayan Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_Campaign "Malayan Campaign"), c.January 1942

After the revolution which established the [Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China_(1912%E2%80%931949) "Republic of China (1912–1949)"), branches of the [Kuomintang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuomintang "Kuomintang") emerged in British Malaya.[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhoo2010-65) Kuomintang activities in British Borneo were coordinated by the Democratic Party of North Borneo, consisting of Chinese-educated [towkays](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/towkay "wikt:towkay").[[67]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorneo_Research_Council1977114-68) Patriotism among Chinese immigrant communities focused on China.[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang1964141-69) British colonial authorities initially did not object to Kuomintang membership.[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHak200069-70) A communist movement developed among overseas Chinese by 1925 with the establishment of the [South Seas Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Seas_Communist_Party "South Seas Communist Party") in Singapore, followed by the [Indochinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indochinese_Communist_Party "Indochinese Communist Party"), [Communist Party of Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Malaya "Communist Party of Malaya"), [Communist Party of Burma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Burma "Communist Party of Burma") and [Communist Party of Siam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Siam "Communist Party of Siam"); the movement also maintained relations with the earlier, Southeast Asian [Communist Party of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Indonesia "Communist Party of Indonesia").[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKennedy1957213-71)[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHara201612%E2%80%9313-72) The Communist Party of Malaya followed the general communist policy of opposing [Western democracies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy "Liberal democracy") before [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II"), increasing propaganda against the Malayan government and the colonial government of Singapore in 1940.[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETay2018-73)

[![Image 25: Outdoor photo of a large group of seated men in uniform](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/The_British_Reoccupation_of_Malaya_SE5883.jpg/250px-The_British_Reoccupation_of_Malaya_SE5883.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_British_Reoccupation_of_Malaya_SE5883.jpg)

[Malayan Peoples' Anti-Japanese Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_Peoples'_Anti-Japanese_Army "Malayan Peoples' Anti-Japanese Army") (MPAJA) guerrillas during their disbandment ceremony in [Kuala Lumpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuala_Lumpur "Kuala Lumpur") after the end of World War II

At the beginning of the Sino-Japanese conflict and the Japanese occupation of [Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_Malaya "Japanese occupation of Malaya") and [British Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_British_Borneo "Japanese occupation of British Borneo"), Kuomintang activities were ended.[[73]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETregonning1962224-74) When the [Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party "Chinese Communist Party") (under [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong "Mao Zedong")) reached an agreement with the Kuomintang government (under [Chiang Kai-shek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Kai-shek "Chiang Kai-shek")) to put aside their differences and rally against Japanese aggression in July 1940, pro-independence sentiment led to bans on the Kuomintang and other Chinese organizations in Malaya.[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETay2018-73)[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang1964144-75) Since the conflict with [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan") also involved the British, Chiang Kai-shek urged Kuomintang members to fight alongside them; in return, British colonial authorities lifted their ban of Chinese associations (including the Kuomintang and the Chinese communist movement).[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheah201337-76) This, and British recognition of the Communist Party of Malaya, resulted in the formation of the [guerrilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla_warfare "Guerrilla warfare")[Malayan Peoples' Anti-Japanese Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_Peoples'_Anti-Japanese_Army "Malayan Peoples' Anti-Japanese Army") (MPAJA) in 1941.[[76]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTESuryadinata2012136-77) The MPAJA (primarily consisting of ethnic Chinese in Malaya) waged a guerrilla war against the [Imperial Japanese Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Japanese_Army "Imperial Japanese Army") (IJA) with 10,000 men in eight battalions, and contacted the British [Force 136](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Force_136 "Force 136").[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong2002a-78) Local Kuomintang revolts, such as the [Jesselton uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesselton_revolt "Jesselton revolt") in British Borneo, were suppressed by the [Kenpeitai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenpeitai "Kenpeitai").[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThurmanSherman2001123-79)[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETay2016-80)

Much Chinese underground anti-Japanese activity in North Borneo was part of the Nanyang Chinese National Salvation Movement, led by [Tan Kah Kee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tan_Kah_Kee "Tan Kah Kee"); in Sarawak, activities were coordinated by the Sarawak Anti-Fascist League.[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEder_Kroef196450%E2%80%9360-81)[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong200193%E2%80%93105-82) The MPAJA was disbanded after the 1945 [Allied victory in the Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_War "Pacific War"), and many of its leaders were commended by the British.[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHartfield2012350-83) It began to massacre perceived collaborators (primarily Malay) after it was disbanded, however, sparking a Malay backlash.[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKantowicz2000111-84) The MPAJA also targeted British posts, with several [grenade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grenade "Grenade") attacks on British troops.[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHartfield2012350-83) In North Borneo, the Chinese co-operated with the British and pledged loyalty to King [George VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_VI "George VI") when the [Crown Colony of North Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_North_Borneo "Crown Colony of North Borneo") was formed after the war.[[84]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalgoorlie_Miner1946-85)

### Postwar unrest and social integration (1946–1962)

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Postwar unrest and social integration (1946–1962)")]

[![Image 26: Two armed police officers questioning an elderly man](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Police_in_Malayan_Emergency.jpg/250px-Police_in_Malayan_Emergency.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Police_in_Malayan_Emergency.jpg)

British and Malayan police talking to a Chinese civilian about communist activity in the area in 1949

> The answer [to the uprising] lies not in pouring more troops into the jungle, but in the hearts and minds of the people.

—Gerald Templer after his appointment as [British High Commissioner for Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_High_Commissioners_of_the_United_Kingdom_for_Malaya "List of High Commissioners of the United Kingdom for Malaya") to deal with the 1952 [Malayan Emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_Emergency "Malayan Emergency")[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELapping1985224-86)

With the MPAJA renamed as the [Malayan National Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_National_Liberation_Army "Malayan National Liberation Army") (MNLA), communist insurgents used guerrilla tactics in the jungles, sabotaged transportation networks and attacked British plantations (assassinating several European plantation owners).[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKantowicz2000111-84) British intelligence estimated MNLA support at about 500,000, out of a total Chinese population of 3.12 million; battling the insurgents was difficult, since most hid in inaccessible jungles.[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKohChoo2009-87) With the 18 June 1948 declaration of the [Malayan Emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_Emergency "Malayan Emergency"), the early government response was chaotic; its primary aim was to guard important economic targets.[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeifer2013110-88)[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOng201487-89) Most Malayan Chinese opposed the MNLA.[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHack199999%E2%80%93125-90)

[![Image 27: Village with a dirt road and closely-spaced houses](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/New_Village_in_Malaya%2C_1950s.jpg/250px-New_Village_in_Malaya%2C_1950s.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:New_Village_in_Malaya,_1950s.jpg)

New villages were established during the 1950s to segregate Chinese with connections to communist insurgents.[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYamada2004225%E2%80%93231-91)

The 1950 [Briggs Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Briggs_Plan "Briggs Plan"), devised by British general [Harold Briggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Rawdon_Briggs "Harold Rawdon Briggs"), intended to isolate the Chinese population from the insurgents. Independence was promised, and supported by the Malays; the Chinese population feared being treated as second-class citizens in an independent Malaya.[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChaliand1982272-92) In addition to new villages, the plan included labour reorganisation, the deployment of troops throughout Malaya, and cooperation between the police, the army, and intelligence.[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamby200259-93) Under the resettlement scheme, more than 700,000 rural Chinese communities were relocated in 582 new government settlements.[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYamada2004225%E2%80%93231-91) After British high commissioner [Henry Gurney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Gurney "Henry Gurney") died in a 1951 ambush, [Gerald Templer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Templer "Gerald Templer") was appointed as the new high commissioner for Malaya by [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill "Winston Churchill") the following year; Templer preferred [winning hearts and minds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winning_hearts_and_minds "Winning hearts and minds") to military measures against the insurgents.[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKohChoo2009-87)[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStubbs20041-94)[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamakrishna2013139-95) As part of his campaign, Templer incentivised rebel surrender and used strict [curfews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curfew "Curfew") and tight control of food supplies in involved areas to flush them out.[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTENolan2011248-96)[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEComber2015100-97) Crops grown by insurgents were sprayed with [herbicides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbicide "Herbicide") and [defoliants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defoliant "Defoliant"), and restrictions were lifted in areas free of insurgents by elevating them to "white status".[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOoi2004830-98)[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200849-99)[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamblin2013175-100) In the [Batang Kali massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batang_Kali_massacre "Batang Kali massacre"), 24 unarmed Malaysian civilians suspected of providing aid to the MNLA were shot by a [Scots Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_Guard "Scots Guard") division in late 1948.[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHale2013432-101)

[![Image 28: Group of people marching with a banner in Chinese and English](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/Anti-communist_rally_in_Semenyih%2C_Selangor_during_the_ensuing_Malayan_Emergency%2C_1950s.jpg/250px-Anti-communist_rally_in_Semenyih%2C_Selangor_during_the_ensuing_Malayan_Emergency%2C_1950s.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Anti-communist_rally_in_Semenyih,_Selangor_during_the_ensuing_Malayan_Emergency,_1950s.jpg)

Joint Chinese-Malay anticommunist demonstration in [Semenyih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semenyih "Semenyih"), Selangor during the 1950s

According to [Chin Peng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chin_Peng "Chin Peng"), the success of Templer's campaign was primarily due to the Briggs Plan's resettlement programme;[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGentile201352-102) Gurney also introduced a quasi-ministerial system, with a bill to increase the number of non-Malays eligible for citizenship after independence.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETarling1992376-103) After the 1949 [Chinese Communist Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Revolution "Chinese Communist Revolution"), foreign missionaries in China were forced to leave; Templer invited them to Malaya to provide [spiritual healing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_healing "Spiritual healing"), medical education and welfare measures to residents of the new government villages.[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoySaha201668-104) By September 1952, every person born in Malaya (including 1.2 million Chinese) was granted full citizenship.[[104]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoySaha201667-105)

[![Image 29: City street with buses, bicyclists and cycle rickshaws](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Photo_of_ancient_Penang_inside_a_room.jpg/250px-Photo_of_ancient_Penang_inside_a_room.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Photo_of_ancient_Penang_inside_a_room.jpg)

[George Town, Penang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Town,_Penang "George Town, Penang") during the 1950s

When the [Federation of Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Malaya "Federation of Malaya") gained independence from the British in 1957, there was still an insurgency.[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreat_Britain._Colonial_Office1957469-106) Chinese communists who surrendered were given a choice of repatriation to China or declaring loyalty to the elected government of Malaya and renouncing communist ideology.[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreat_Britain._Colonial_Office19574-107) The anti-communist campaign continued until 1960, when the Malayan government declared the end of its [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_emergency "State of emergency") after Chin Peng demobilized his forces and left his hide-out in southern [Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thailand "Thailand") for [Beijing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing "Beijing") via [North Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Vietnam "North Vietnam").[[107]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTESingh201439-108)[[108]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarver2015200-109) At a meeting of Chin and [Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping "Deng Xiaoping") in Beijing, China urged the Communist Party of Malaya to continue their armed struggle.[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTESingh201441-110) The establishment of a communist government in [mainland China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_China "Mainland China") affected Chinese youth in Sarawak, who organized the pro-Beijing Sarawak Overseas Chinese Democratic Youth League (later the Sarawak Advanced Youth Association, which sparked the 1960 [communist insurgency in Sarawak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_insurgency_in_Sarawak "Communist insurgency in Sarawak").[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEder_Kroef196450%E2%80%9360-81) Malaysia's economy continued to prosper, dependent on tin and rubber industries dominated by a Chinese and Indian workforce.[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGomes200746-111)[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank2010-112)

### Struggle for equality in "Malaysian Malaysia" (1963–1965)

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Struggle for equality in \"Malaysian Malaysia\" (1963–1965)")]

See also: [Malaysian Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Malaysia "Malaysian Malaysia")

Early in 1961, when [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Malaysia "Prime Minister of Malaysia")[Tunku Abdul Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunku_Abdul_Rahman "Tunku Abdul Rahman") outlined a "Grand Malaysian Alliance" of the British protectorate of [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei "Brunei") and the crown colonies of North Borneo, [Sarawak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_Sarawak "Crown Colony of Sarawak") and [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Singapore "Colony of Singapore"),[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunnell200476-113) Singapore [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Singapore "Prime Minister of Singapore")[Lee Kuan Yew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Kuan_Yew "Lee Kuan Yew") supported the proposal. Lee's [People's Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Action_Party "People's Action Party") (PAP) rallied for the equality of all Malaysians, regardless of "class, skin colour or creed", known as "[Malaysian Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Malaysia "Malaysian Malaysia")", in a multi-ethnic society without [Malayisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayisation "Malayisation").[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPurushotam2000209-114)[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJosey2013153-115) Although Lee was seen by Malay extremists in the [United Malays National Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Malays_National_Organisation "United Malays National Organisation") (UMNO) and the [Pan-Malaysian Islamic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Malaysian_Islamic_Party "Pan-Malaysian Islamic Party") (PAS), as opposed to _[Ketuanan Melayu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketuanan\_Melayu "Ketuanan Melayu")_ after the 1963 formation of Malaysia,[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheah200254-116)[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkbarzadehSaeed2003142-117) he had adopted [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language "Malay language") as Singapore's national language and appointed Malay [Yusof Ishak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusof_Ishak "Yusof Ishak") as [Yang di-Pertuan Negara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yang_di-Pertuan_Negara_of_Singapore "Yang di-Pertuan Negara of Singapore").[[117]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan2004b42-118)

The [Malaysian Chinese Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese_Association "Malaysian Chinese Association") (MCA), led by [Tan Cheng Lock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tan_Cheng_Lock "Tan Cheng Lock") and part of the [Malaysian Alliance Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_Party_(Malaysia) "Alliance Party (Malaysia)"), refused to join the struggle for equality. The MCA feared that equality would disrupt an agreement between the [United Malays National Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Malays_National_Organisation "United Malays National Organisation") (UMNO) and the [Malaysian Indian Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Indian_Congress "Malaysian Indian Congress") (MIC) in which Malays were politically dominant and the Chinese controlled the country's economy; [Malaysian Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Indians "Malaysian Indians") played a smaller economic role, with the Malays promising to share future political power with the other two groups.[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOoi200455-119) After two years as part of the federation and the enactment of [Article 153 of the Constitution of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_153_of_the_Constitution_of_Malaysia "Article 153 of the Constitution of Malaysia"), Lee was joined by the Malay Peninsula's [United Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Democratic_Party_(Malaysia) "United Democratic Party (Malaysia)") and [People's Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Progressive_Party_(Malaysia) "People's Progressive Party (Malaysia)") and Sarawak's [United Peoples' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarawak_United_Peoples'_Party "Sarawak United Peoples' Party") and Machinda Party in the [Malaysian Solidarity Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Solidarity_Convention "Malaysian Solidarity Convention") to campaign for equality.[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheah200254-116) In a June 1965 speech at the MSC meeting in Singapore, Lee said:

> It took a long time for us all to come to the same conclusions despite our differing experiences in Malaya, Singapore, [Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah "Sabah") and [Sarawak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarawak "Sarawak"). Our friends in Sabah are not here today officially, but I can assure you that we have many friends there. There is no doubt that it took us a long time to reach the inevitable conclusion that these people [referring to the racial politics in Malay Peninsula] were up to no good. You know the line they were taking with growing truculence on a heavy racial accent, the intimidatory postures and the snarling guttural notes on which they sent out their signals to their followers on the basis of the race lead us to only one conclusion -- that if this goes on, Malaysia will not belong to [Malaysians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysians "Malaysians"). Since there are so many Malaysians, we decided that the time has come for us to speak our minds.[[119]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_of_Singapore19651-120)

Lee's call for a re-alignment of forces between those who wanted a "true Malaysian nation" and those who preferred a country led by a component of the Alliance Party, and his criticism of Malays advocating Malay dominance, led to bitter [PAP–UMNO relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PAP%E2%80%93UMNO_relations "PAP–UMNO relations") and the [1964 race riots in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_race_riots_in_Singapore "1964 race riots in Singapore"); Singapore was later [expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_in_Malaysia "Singapore in Malaysia") from the federation in August 1965, and became its own sovereign nation.[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheah200254-116)[[120]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiamondPlattnerChu2013187-121)[[121]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid2015362-122)

### Communist insurgencies and racial clashes (1965–1990)

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: Communist insurgencies and racial clashes (1965–1990)")]

The struggle for equality continued with the [Democratic Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Action_Party "Democratic Action Party") (DAP), which succeeded the PAP in 1965.[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkbarzadehSaeed2003142-117) Many Malaysian Chinese began to view the Malaysian Chinese Association as more concerned with business and economic interests than social factors, although Prime Minister Tunku Abdul Rahman considered the MCA the sole legitimate representative of the federation's Chinese community.[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkbarzadehSaeed2003142-117) In 1968, communists supported by China began [a new insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_insurgency_in_Malaysia_(1968%E2%80%931989) "Communist insurgency in Malaysia (1968–1989)") from their stronghold in southern Thailand.[[122]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTERazak201569-123)[[123]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOng201495-124)[The following year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Malaysian_general_election "1969 Malaysian general election"), the MCA was challenged by the DAP and the [Malaysian People's Movement Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parti_Gerakan_Rakyat_Malaysia "Parti Gerakan Rakyat Malaysia") (GERAKAN). Of its 33 parliamentary seats contested in the election, the MCA retained 13 and lost control of the [Penang state government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Penang "Government of Penang") to GERAKAN; Chinese-Malay tensions culminated in the [13 May incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/13_May_incident "13 May incident").[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECharlesCheng197147-125)[[125]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEder_Kroef1981107-126) Other clashes were also ethnic in nature.[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKua200774-127)

In the aftermath of the riots, Prime Minister Tunku Abdul Rahman blamed the communists rather than racial issues and said that the [Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War "Vietnam War") in their neighbour was "not simply a civil war but [communist-ideology expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domino_theory "Domino theory")".[[127]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheng200966-128) The Malaysian government introduced several policy initiatives in security and development, a neighbourhood-watch program and the [People's Volunteer Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Volunteers_Corps_Department "Malaysia Volunteers Corps Department").[[128]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTENazar200519%E2%80%9320-129) The Communist Party of Malaya split into two factions during the early 1970s: a [revolutionary wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Malaya/Revolutionary_Faction "Communist Party of Malaya/Revolutionary Faction") in 1970 and a [Marxist–Leninist wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Malaya/Marxist%E2%80%93Leninist "Communist Party of Malaya/Marxist–Leninist") in 1974.[[129]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChin2003467%E2%80%93468-130)[Kuala Lumpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuala_Lumpur "Kuala Lumpur") and Beijing established [diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Malaysia_relations "China–Malaysia relations") in 1974; China ended its aid to the factions, prompting their 1983 merger into the [Malaysian Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Communist_Party "Malaysian Communist Party") before their surrender to Thai authorities four years later and the [1989 peace agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Agreement_of_Hat_Yai_(1989) "Peace Agreement of Hat Yai (1989)") ending the insurgency.[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhoo2010-65)[[130]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChin2003450-131) A Chinese communist insurgency in Sarawak, supported by neighbouring Indonesia, ended with peace negotiations in 1990.[[131]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPorritt2004279-132)

### 1990 to present

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: 1990 to present")]

[![Image 30: City street with heavy traffic, orange overhead lanterns, and mountains in the background](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Chinese_New_Year_at_Ayer_Itam%2C_George_Town%2C_Penang_2024.jpg/250px-Chinese_New_Year_at_Ayer_Itam%2C_George_Town%2C_Penang_2024.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Chinese_New_Year_at_Ayer_Itam,_George_Town,_Penang_2024.jpg)

[Chinese New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_New_Year "Chinese New Year") decorations in [George Town, Penang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Town,_Penang "George Town, Penang")

[![Image 31: Crowded city street, with red lanterns](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Petaling_Street%2C_Kuala_Lumpur.jpg/250px-Petaling_Street%2C_Kuala_Lumpur.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Petaling_Street,_Kuala_Lumpur.jpg)

[Petaling Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petaling_Street "Petaling Street"), a [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown "Chinatown") in Malaysia's capital of [Kuala Lumpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuala_Lumpur "Kuala Lumpur"), at night

Issues between the Malaysian Chinese and the Malays remain unresolved.[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETong201095-133) The failure of [Malaysia's social contract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_contract_(Malaysia) "Social contract (Malaysia)") has led to a strong [Chinese identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_identity "Chinese identity"), in contrast to the Chinese communities in neighbouring [Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Indonesians "Chinese Indonesians"), [the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Filipino "Chinese Filipino"), [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Singaporeans "Chinese Singaporeans"), [Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_Chinese "Thai Chinese") and [Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoa_people "Hoa people").[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETong201095-133)[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChen200466-134) After the 1969 racial clashes, the [Malaysian New Economic Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_New_Economic_Policy "Malaysian New Economic Policy") favouring the Malays from 1971 to 1990 increased _[Bumiputera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bumiputera\_(Malaysia) "Bumiputera (Malaysia)")_ economic control by 60 percent.[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGomez20121-135) Malaysian Chinese remain the business sector's dominant players; [equity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equity_(finance) "Equity (finance)") ownership doubled from 22.8 percent in 1969 to 45.5 percent in 1990, and nearly all of [Malaysia's richest people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Malaysians_by_net_worth "List of Malaysians by net worth") are Chinese.[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-136)

Since Malaysian Chinese manage the country's economy, most (75.8 percent in 1991) live in urban areas.[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamTan200078-137) They generally do not speak Malay among themselves;[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWangWang199831-138) this contrasts with earlier Chinese communities such as the Straits-born Chinese of Baba-Nyonya, Kelantanese and Terengganuan [Peranakans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peranakans "Peranakans"), [Penangite Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penangite_Chinese "Penangite Chinese") and Sabah [Sino-natives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Native "Sino-Native"), who mingled with local Malay and other indigenous peoples.[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan200493%E2%80%9395-139)[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong200455-140)[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETong2010110-141) Chinese in [East Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Malaysia "East Malaysia") in Sabah and Sarawak, particularly in small inland towns, interact more closely with the indigenous communities.[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETong2010110-141) Integration issues in present-day Malaysia are largely inherited from Malayan racial politics, in which ethnic communities consolidated into a single political community.[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnloe1968372%E2%80%93385-142) Malaysia's contemporary [educational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Malaysia "Education in Malaysia") and social policies has created a Chinese [brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital_flight "Human capital flight") to [developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_countries "Developed countries"), especially [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore "Singapore").[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJakobsen2014170-143)[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETyson201855-144) The country has experienced a slight wave of [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese "Mandarin Chinese")-speaking immigrants from [northeastern China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeastern_China "Northeastern China") and a smaller number of [Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_people "Vietnamese people") immigrants, however, with local men marrying women from China and [Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam "Vietnam").[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhor2016-145)

## Origins and social demographics

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: Origins and social demographics")]

See also: [Chinese emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_emigration "Chinese emigration") and [Overseas Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Chinese "Overseas Chinese")

[![Image 32](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Origin_of_ethnic_Chinese_Malaysians.svg/250px-Origin_of_ethnic_Chinese_Malaysians.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Origin_of_ethnic_Chinese_Malaysians.svg)

Map of the geographic origin of the present-day ethnic Chinese Malaysians since their early migrations from China to [Nanyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanyang_(region) "Nanyang (region)") region more than a hundred years ago.[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhor2016-145)

Since their early ancient trade connection and subsequent migrations, the majority of ethnic Chinese in Malaysia are [ethnic Han](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese "Han Chinese") from the historical areas of [Fujian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujian "Fujian") and [Guangdong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangdong "Guangdong") provinces in [southern China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_China "Southern China").[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTESimon199651-146) Nearly all ethnic Chinese Malaysians living in the country today are either [patrilineal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrilineality "Patrilineality") descendants of these early immigrants, or new immigrants born in mainland China.[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan1997103%E2%80%93116-147) Among the first group from the first migration wave are the Peranakans in [Kelantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelantan "Kelantan") and [Terengganu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terengganu "Terengganu"), Sino-Natives in [Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah "Sabah") and Straits-born Chinese (Baba-Nyonya) in [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca "Malacca") and [Penang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penang "Penang").[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan200493%E2%80%9395-139)[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong200455-140)[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOxford_Business_Group200812-148) The [Taiping Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiping_Rebellion "Taiping Rebellion") in the mid-19th century caused much chaos and suffering in southern China, which led many ethnic Chinese civilians to seek refuge overseas, particularly to the [Nanyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanyang_(region) "Nanyang (region)") region (Southeast Asia) in further south.[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004698-149) With the expanding British colonial ambitions in the region which led to the establishment of [Straits Settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straits_Settlements "Straits Settlements"), more migrants were attracted and the region became an important destination for [Chinese emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_emigration "Chinese emigration").[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004698-149)

[![Image 33](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Southern_Chinese_%28From_Fukien%29%2C_Merchant_in_Penang.JPG/250px-Southern_Chinese_%28From_Fukien%29%2C_Merchant_in_Penang.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Southern_Chinese_(From_Fukien),_Merchant_in_Penang.JPG)

An 1880 painting of southern Chinese merchant from [Fujian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujian "Fujian") (left) and Chinese official in [Penang Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penang_Island "Penang Island").

By the late 19th century with the second wave of migration through the British colonial rule, the [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien_people "Hokkien people") of southern Fujian led by many of their wealthy and powerful individuals with skills in [finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance "Finance") and [maritime shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_shipping "Maritime shipping") became the dominant immigrant group as the first group of ethnic Chinese to settle in large numbers with their language become a [contact language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contact_language "Contact language") among Chinese of different linguistics background.[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-150) The [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese_people "Cantonese people") from Guangdong follow suit and became well known as mineworkers, [land reclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reclamation "Land reclamation"), mechanics and their familiarity with [cash cropping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cash_crop "Cash crop").[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReidRodgers200182-151)[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuhn200847-152) Another large group, the [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_people "Hakka people") are mostly hill farmers originated from northeastern Guangdong and various southern China places who specialise in forest clearance, mining as well on [metalworking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metalworking "Metalworking").[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReidRodgers200182-151)[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuhn200847-152) Other groups like the [Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_people "Teochew people") from [Chaozhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaozhou "Chaozhou") of eastern Guangdong are mainly on [plantation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation "Plantation")
