agriculture,[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReidRodgers200182-151)[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuhn200847-152) the [Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_people "Fuzhou people") from central Fujian specialise in [entrepreneurship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entrepreneurship "Entrepreneurship"),[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaur2016194-153) while the [Hainanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainanese_people "Hainanese people") from [Hainan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainan "Hainan") as one of the ethnic Chinese minority saw themselves as [sojourners](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/sojourner "wikt:sojourner") with many of Hainanese chefs and waiters ruled the kitchen of local Chinese [kopi tiam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kopi_tiam "Kopi tiam") and [restaurants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restaurant "Restaurant").[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan200220-154)[[154]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTESharp20081-155) The [Henghua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putian_people "Putian people") and Hokchia from eastern Fujian are usually managing family industries while the Kwongsai from [Guangxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangxi "Guangxi") are employed in labour sectors.[[155]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalaysian_Historical_Society196934-156)[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDouwPost1996148-157) Followed by the third wave of ethnic Chinese migration thereafter, this resulted from Malaysia as being one of the countries outside China and [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan "Taiwan") with [largest population of Chinese speakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_cultural_sphere "East Asian cultural sphere") in the world.[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhor2016-145)[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsmah200452-158) Following the establishment of diplomatic relationship between Mainland China and Malaysia in 1974, a [joint communiqué](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/joint%20communiqu%C3%A9 "wikt:joint communiqué") was made between the two nations, which stated:

> The [Chinese government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_China "Government of China") considers anyone of [Chinese origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_people "Chinese people") who has taken up his own will or acquired [Malaysian nationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_nationality_law "Malaysian nationality law") as automatically forfeiting [Chinese nationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationality_Law_of_China "Nationality Law of China"). As for residents who retain Chinese nationality of their own will, the Chinese government, acting in accordance with its consistent policy, will enjoin them to abide by the law of the [Government of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Malaysia "Government of Malaysia"), respect the customs and habits of the people there, and live in unity with them, and their proper rights and interests will be protected by the Government of China and respected by the Government of Malaysia.[[158]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECamilleri197527-159)

Different [varieties of Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Chinese "Varieties of Chinese") are spoken in Malaysian cities and towns. Across the country, [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Mandarin "Malaysian Mandarin") has emerged as the _[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua\_franca "Lingua franca")_ among the various Chinese subgroups, largely due to its use as the language of instruction in Chinese schools and [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China")'s increasing economic influence.[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-UN-160)

Within [Peninsular Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_Malaysia "Peninsular Malaysia"), [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien "Hokkien") is predominantly spoken in west coast cities such as [George Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Town,_Penang "George Town, Penang"), [Seberang Perai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seberang_Perai "Seberang Perai"), [Klang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klang_(city) "Klang (city)"), [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca_City "Malacca City"), [Batu Pahat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batu_Pahat_(town) "Batu Pahat (town)") and [Muar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muar_(town) "Muar (town)").[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabacc%C3%ADaHoerder2011217%E2%80%93218-161)[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2007699-162)[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEArmstrongArmstrongMulliner2012221-163) In contrast, [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese "Cantonese") is the main Chinese language in [Kuala Lumpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuala_Lumpur "Kuala Lumpur"), [Ipoh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipoh "Ipoh"), [Seremban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seremban "Seremban") and [Kuantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuantan "Kuantan").[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabacc%C3%ADaHoerder2011217%E2%80%93218-161)[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2007699-162) Interior towns like [Cameron Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameron_Highlands "Cameron Highlands") and [Kulai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kulai "Kulai") were primarily [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_Chinese "Hakka Chinese")-speaking.[[163]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReview_Publishing_Company_Limited197416-164)[[164]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-165)[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-166)[Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_dialect "Teochew dialect") was also commonly used along the western coast of [Perak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perak "Perak") and in [Johor Bahru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor_Bahru "Johor Bahru").[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabacc%C3%ADaHoerder2011217%E2%80%93218-161)[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2007699-162)[Bentong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bentong "Bentong") and [Raub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raub,_Pahang "Raub, Pahang") contained a substantial Kwongsai-speaking population.[[166]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeng198883-167)

The distribution of Chinese languages in [East Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Malaysia "East Malaysia") exhibits significant variation. In [Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah "Sabah"), Hakka is the predominant language, particularly in [Kota Kinabalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Kinabalu "Kota Kinabalu"), [Lahad Datu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahad_Datu "Lahad Datu"), [Semporna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semporna "Semporna"), [Papar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papar,_Malaysia "Papar, Malaysia") and [Tawau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawau "Tawau").[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabacc%C3%ADaHoerder2011217%E2%80%93218-161)[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPan1998183%E2%80%93184-168)[[168]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTE%E6%9E%97%E9%96%8B%E5%BF%A0201370-169) In [Sarawak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarawak "Sarawak"), Hakka is also prevalent in towns such as [Serian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serian_Division "Serian Division"), [Kota Samarahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Samarahan "Kota Samarahan") and [Bau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bau,_Sarawak "Bau, Sarawak").[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabacc%C3%ADaHoerder2011217%E2%80%93218-161)[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPan1998183%E2%80%93184-168)[[169]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-170) Cantonese serves as the main language in [Miri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miri "Miri") and [Sandakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandakan "Sandakan").[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabacc%C3%ADaHoerder2011217%E2%80%93218-161)[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuan2012114-171)[Kuching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuching "Kuching") and [Kapit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapit "Kapit") contained multiple language groups, including Hokkien, Hakka and [Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_dialect "Fuzhou dialect").[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuan2012114-171)[[171]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-172) The town of [Sibu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sibu "Sibu") was often referred to as a mini-[Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou "Fuzhou"), with the language also being used in [Sarikei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarikei "Sarikei").[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2007699-162)[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuan2012114-171)

[![Image 50](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Geographical_distributions_of_ethnic_Chinese_Malaysians.svg/250px-Geographical_distributions_of_ethnic_Chinese_Malaysians.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Geographical_distributions_of_ethnic_Chinese_Malaysians.svg)

Geographical distributions of ethnic Chinese Malaysians by majority in each cities or towns:  

[Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien_people "Hokkien people")

[Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese_people "Cantonese people")

[Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_people "Hakka people")

[Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_people "Fuzhou people")

[Hainanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainanese_people "Hainanese people")

[Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_people "Teochew people")

[Kwongsai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangxi "Guangxi")

[Hockchia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuqing_dialect "Fuqing dialect")

Undetermined majority

The first census conducted in 1970 after Malaysia was formed in 1963 reported that there were 3,555,879 ethnic Chinese Malaysians, with the [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien_people "Hokkien people") being the majority at 32.4%, followed by [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_people "Hakka people") at 22.1%, [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese_people "Cantonese people") at 19.8%, [Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_people "Teochew people") and [Hainanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainanese_people "Hainanese people") at 12.4% and 4.7% respectively.[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang201212-173) This increase to 4,554,664 in 1980 and 4,623,900 in 1991.[[173]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYamaguchiDeterding20164-174) In 2000, the census reported that ethnic Chinese Malaysians numbered at 5,691,908, which was approximately 24.45% of the country's population at the time, with 86% of them living in urban areas.[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004698-149) In [West Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Malaysia "West Malaysia"), Hokkien became the leading group followed by Cantonese, Hakka and Teochew.[[174]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamTan200039-175) The pattern differs in East Malaysia where Hakka is the majority in Sabah and Sarawak followed by Hokkien and Cantonese in Sabah and the [Fuzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_people "Fuzhou people"), Hokkien and Teochew in Sarawak.[[175]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamTan200047-176)[[176]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTET'ien1983278-177)[[177]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYong2013291-178) The population increased further to 6,392,636 by 2010 and 6,892,367 by 2020.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-DOSM2020-1)[[178]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDepartment_of_Statistics_Malaysia2010a15-179)As of 2020[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit), over three-fifths of Malaysia's ethnic Chinese resided in [Selangor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selangor "Selangor"), [Johor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor "Johor"), [Kuala Lumpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuala_Lumpur "Kuala Lumpur") and [Penang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penang "Penang"). Penang also had the largest percentage of Chinese and was the only state where the Chinese constituted a plurality.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-DOSM2020-1)

There is also a small community of _[Huáběi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu%C3%A1b%C4%9Bi "Huáběi")-ren_ (Northern Chinese) in Sabah whose ancestors coming from various parts of [Hebei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebei "Hebei") and [Shandong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shandong "Shandong") provinces in China with most of them calling themselves as _[Tiānjīn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ti%C4%81nj%C4%ABn "Tiānjīn")-ren_. Besides that, Sanjiang-ren ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language "Chinese language"):[三江人](https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E4%B8%89%E6%B1%9F%E4%BA%BA "zh:三江人")) is a collective term used to categorise the group of people whose ancestors hailed from [Shanghai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai "Shanghai"), [Zhejiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhejiang "Zhejiang"), [Jiangsu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiangsu "Jiangsu"), [Hubei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubei "Hubei"), and [Jiangxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiangxi "Jiangxi"). It is a combined group of different spoken varieties and has the fewest people as compared to other Chinese subgroups. The first San Jiang Clansmen Association or San Kiang Association was formed in Penang back in 1897.[[179]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPenang_San_Kiang_Association2020-180)[[180]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004699-181)

Although the ethnic Chinese population had been increasing since the 1970s, their proportion within the total population had been decreasing gradually, particularly caused by the lower [birth rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_rate "Birth rate") among the Chinese community.[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang201212-173)

### Economic, demographic and political representation

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=12 "Edit section: Economic, demographic and political representation")]

See also: [Economy of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Malaysia "Economy of Malaysia") and [Bamboo network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamboo_network "Bamboo network")

[![Image 51](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Malaysia_election_results_map_by_ethnicity%2C_2018.svg/500px-Malaysia_election_results_map_by_ethnicity%2C_2018.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Malaysia_election_results_map_by_ethnicity,_2018.svg)

Proportion of ethnic Chinese and Malay with indigenous voters in the [2018 Malaysian parliamentary constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Results_of_the_2018_Malaysian_general_election_by_parliamentary_constituency "Results of the 2018 Malaysian general election by parliamentary constituency"):  

Bright red indicates a higher proportion of ethnic Chinese

Bright green indicates a higher proportion of [ethnic Malay or indigenous people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bumiputera_(Malaysia) "Bumiputera (Malaysia)")

Mixed colour means that the ratio of the two is equivalent

The brownish colour are the area with the higher proportion of other non-indigenous races (such as [Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Indians "Malaysian Indians"))

Ethnic Chinese Malaysians have been traditionally dominant in the business sector of the Malaysian economy with large local Chinese enterprises involved in [natural resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_resources "Natural resources") and food ingredients industries such as [sugar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar "Sugar") and [palm oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_oil "Palm oil").[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoon2007234-182) Almost every [biscuits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biscuit "Biscuit") manufacturer in the country is dominated by ethnic Chinese Malaysians.[[182]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChiam2015-183) Up to the 1970s, their economic structure was intertwined with very much family and [kinship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinship "Kinship") ties.[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoon2007195-184) By the 21st century with the [rising of China's economic influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_China "Economy of China"), their [economy network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_network "Business network") are jointly connected with other [Overseas Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Chinese "Overseas Chinese") through the [bamboo network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamboo_network "Bamboo network").[[184]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeidenbaumHughes199647-185) The local Chinese played a key role in facilitating China's [capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_(economics) "Capital (economics)") to invest in Malaysia while in the process both benefited from expanded markets, lower labour costs and the introduction of different kind of technologies and managerial systems which resulted from Malaysia becoming the largest trading partner to China in [Association of Southeast Asian Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Southeast_Asian_Nations "Association of Southeast Asian Nations") (ASEAN) region.[[185]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWongTan2017113-186) Nevertheless, unlike the more transboundary business influence by neighbouring [Chinese Singaporeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Singaporeans "Chinese Singaporeans"), a majority of Chinese Malaysian businesses are still seen as less concentrating on developing business ties with other Southeast Asian Chinese businessmen despite there have been few businesses made by several local Malaysian Chinese companies.[[186]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGomez2012185-187)

Since the country's foundation in 1963, the [Malaysian Chinese Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese_Association "Malaysian Chinese Association") (MCA) was the sole legitimate political representation for ethnic Chinese in Malaysia under the multi-racial political coalition of the [Alliance Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_Party_(Malaysia) "Alliance Party (Malaysia)") (later expanded into [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barisan_Nasional "Barisan Nasional") coalition).[[187]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECartier201171-188) However, the growing restriction of non-Malay interests in Malaysian society and politics since the 1970s caused deep dissension among Chinese Malaysians. The perception that their ethnic party representatives were unable to stand for their people's rights grew, and that decision-making was influenced by the Malay [supremacists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supremacism "Supremacism") dominated party of [United Malays National Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Malays_National_Organisation "United Malays National Organisation") (UMNO). The UMNO was the most influential part of the existing political coalition, and also championed Islamic values.[[188]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-189) Despite the coalition's multi-racial appearance, UMNO founding president [Onn Jaafar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onn_Jaafar "Onn Jaafar") has once said that their movement did not adhere to any ideology other than "[Malayism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayisation "Malayisation")", defined by Malay scholar [Ariffin Omar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariffin_Omar "Ariffin Omar") as the belief that the interests of the Malay race must be upheld over anything else.[[189]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETing200935-190) From 1968, the inability of MCA to preserve ethnic Chinese Malaysian interests, particularly with regards to their culture, education, and language, resulted in the formation of another ethnic Chinese representative party, the [Malaysian People's Movement Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parti_Gerakan_Rakyat_Malaysia "Parti Gerakan Rakyat Malaysia") (GERAKAN) led by Chinese overseas-educated elite, although this party also joined the Alliance Party coalition in 1972.[[190]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJenkins200891-191)

The growing [Islamisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamisation "Islamisation") with "Islamic version of non-tolerant and open", increasing [racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_in_Malaysia "Racism in Malaysia") within the Malay party members throughout the coalition imposing further into the country social demographics as well as the government discriminative policies on their education and employment, caused further disenchantment among non-Malays, especially ethnic Chinese.[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-192)[[192]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-193) This subsequently caused dwindling support from ethnic Chinese Malaysians when many original strongholds of MCA [constituents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Malaysian_electoral_districts "List of Malaysian electoral districts") and even Gerakan turned to the [Democratic Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Action_Party "Democratic Action Party") (DAP) as the latter were seen as more vocal on their rights.[[193]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeiner%C3%96zbudun1987136-194)[[194]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoplin19919-195)[[195]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECase2013105-196) The [recent country general election in 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Malaysian_general_election "2018 Malaysian general election") saw the fall of National Front coalition following additional aid from Bornean parliamentary seats particularly in Sabah that were won by [Pakatan Harapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakatan_Harapan "Pakatan Harapan") coalition ally of [regionalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regionalism_(politics) "Regionalism (politics)")[Sabah Heritage Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Heritage_Party "Sabah Heritage Party") (WARISAN) as well from another indigenous party of [United Pasokmomogun Kadazandusun Murut Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Pasokmomogun_Kadazandusun_Murut_Organisation "United Pasokmomogun Kadazandusun Murut Organisation") (UPKO) as a result of similar growing frustration over long-time manipulation of the state demographics and resources with lack of development through the administration of the Malay-led supremacists government of UMNO with frequent labelling as "fixed deposit state".[[196]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTESipalanMenon2018-197)[[197]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Borneo_Post2019-198)[[198]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2018-199) During the election, the DAP won a large amount of seats due to the support of many Chinese constituents and subsequently diminished MCA influence among ethnic Chinese Malaysians.[[199]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChengMokhtar2018-200)[[200]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDayley2018203-201)[[201]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTESukumaran2019-202)

Although the Malaysian Chinese population is increasing in every [census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Census "Census"), the proportion of ethnic Chinese among the country's total population has been declining in recent decades because of a lower [birth rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_rate "Birth rate") and high [emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration "Emigration") rate. There are significant concerns about the loss of political representation. According to a report by the [World Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank "World Bank"), the [Malaysian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_diaspora "Malaysian diaspora") around the world in 2010 included around a million people, most of them ethnic Chinese. The large number of emigrants, many of whom are young with education or skills, constitutes a significant "[brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital_flight "Human capital flight")" from the country, especially towards the country's immediate neighbour [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore "Singapore").[[202]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-203)

### Traditional Chinese Medicine in Malaysia

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Traditional Chinese Medicine in Malaysia")]

See also: [Traditional Chinese medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_medicine "Traditional Chinese medicine")

[![Image 52](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Traditional_Chinese_Medicine_Shop%2C_George_Town%2C_Penang.jpg/250px-Traditional_Chinese_Medicine_Shop%2C_George_Town%2C_Penang.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Traditional_Chinese_Medicine_Shop,_George_Town,_Penang.jpg)

An ethnic Chinese woman in Malaysia grinds and cuts up dried [herbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_herbology "Chinese herbology") to make [traditional Chinese medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_medicine "Traditional Chinese medicine").

Ethnic Chinese Malaysians have contributed to the spread of traditional Chinese medicine in Malaysia. Around 3,000 medicine shops in the country sell traditional Chinese medicine, and there is some regulation under the [Health Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Health_(Malaysia) "Ministry of Health (Malaysia)").[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStevensonTaiYuan2014282-204) Chinese traditional health practice services in the country generally encompass [herbal medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_herbology "Chinese herbology"), _[qigong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qigong "Qigong")_, [chiropractic care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiropractic_care "Chiropractic care"), [acupuncture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acupuncture "Acupuncture"), and other techniques.[[204]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeh2017-205) In 2012, the Traditional and Complementary Medicine Bill (T&CM) was passed by the [Parliament of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Malaysia "Parliament of Malaysia") for the establishment of "Traditional and Complementary Medicine Council or Majlis Perubatan Tradisional dan Komplementari" to register and regulate traditional and complementary medicine practitioners, including traditional Chinese medicine practitioners as well Malay, indigenous and Indians traditional medicine, which was followed by the enforcement of T&CM Act in the following year.[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinistry_of_Health_Malaysia201510-206) Most traditional-medicine pharmacies are family-apprentice trades. While the Health Ministry has tolerated the development of TCM, it does not enforce registration of traditional-medicine practitioners, who are nevertheless allowed to practice the trade.[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStevensonTaiYuan2014282-204)

### Education

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section: Education")]

See also: [Chinese independent high school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_independent_high_school "Chinese independent high school") and [Education in Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Malaysia "Education in Malaysia")

[![Image 53](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Sabah_Tshung_Tsin_Secondary_School.JPG/250px-Sabah_Tshung_Tsin_Secondary_School.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sabah_Tshung_Tsin_Secondary_School.JPG)

Co-curricular activities in [Tshung Tsin Secondary School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Tshung_Tsin_Secondary_School "Sabah Tshung Tsin Secondary School"), a [Chinese independent high school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_independent_high_school "Chinese independent high school") in [Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah "Sabah").

In educational aspects, Malaysia is the only country outside China and [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan "Taiwan") with a comprehensive and complete Chinese education system and the only Southeast Asian country that has perpetuated the Chinese education system established since the colonial era as a result of heavy brokerage and lobbying efforts by ethnic Chinese Malaysian political leaders with continual funding from local Chinese communities.[[206]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECurdt-ChristiansenHancock2014145-207)[[207]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAng2017-208) The first Chinese schools had been established in the country in the 19th century during the British colonial administration.[[208]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamTan2000230-209)[[209]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong2002b35-210)[[210]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECharneyYeohTong2003147-211) At the same time, Christian missions from China also founded schools for Chinese students to nurture Church workers, this are most notable among many Chinese Christians of Hakka origin in Sabah and the Fuzhou in Sarawak.[[211]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-212) Following the country's independence, some of the Chinese independent schools who were influenced by the new national agenda and desperately needed government financial support were willingly converted into [English medium-schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English-medium_education "English-medium education"). However, these schools were later scrupulously converted into [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language "Malay language") medium-schools following the massive elimination of English medium-schools by the Malay-dominated regime in the 1970s in an effort to impose the Malay language as the only medium of instructions in all schools in the aftermath of ethnic riots in 1969.[[206]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECurdt-ChristiansenHancock2014145-207)[[207]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAng2017-208) The following period was considered a dark time for local Chinese education and witnessed a growing belief in the idea that the ethnic Chinese in Malaysia had to proceed on a basis of self-reliance and vigilance in order to preserve their language rights as the national education policy was seen as exclusionary and discriminatory for non-Malays, summed up in the aphorism "non-Malays belong to Malaysia but Malaysia only belongs to the Malays".[[206]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECurdt-ChristiansenHancock2014145-207)[[212]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeeHillYin201675-213)

[![Image 54](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/SMJK_Confucian%2C_100-year_anniversary.jpg/250px-SMJK_Confucian%2C_100-year_anniversary.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SMJK_Confucian,_100-year_anniversary.jpg)

SMJK Confucian in Kuala Lumpur, an example of converted Chinese independent school to government-funded "SMJK" ([National-type Chinese secondary school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National-type_Chinese_secondary_school "National-type Chinese secondary school")).

Although other remaining Chinese independent schools ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language "Chinese language"):独中) were also included in the national school system in 1996, these independent schools still did not receive any financial assistance from the federal government and its United Examinations Certificate (UEC; [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language "Chinese language"):统考) is not recognised by the government, making students who finish their studies from the local Chinese independent schools unable to enroll in government-funded public [tertiary institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education "Tertiary education").[[213]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeeHillYin201677-214) Since late 1970, ethnic Chinese Malaysians had expanded their own Mandarin-language primary schools where around 90% of their children were enrolled based on the figures from 2006.[[214]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTENelsonMeermanRahman2008201-215) These schools gained a reputation for not only maintaining good discipline but also providing the high-quality education that the Malay schools were seen by some to lack. This perceived superiority attracted significant interest among [Bumiputera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bumiputera_(Malaysia) "Bumiputera (Malaysia)") parents with various analyses placing the number of Bumiputera students enrollment in Chinese schools between 60,000 and 100,000 in 2000.[[214]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTENelsonMeermanRahman2008201-215) The federal government's failure to impose Malay as the sole unifying language throughout the country's educational systems unlike in China with [Standard Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chinese "Standard Chinese") or Indonesia with [Indonesian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_language "Indonesian language") are mainly attributed to the racial-based policy enforcement of ethnic inclusion and exclusion.[[215]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeeHillYin201678-216) Some argue that the educational policies imposed by the federal government over the years in regard to minority language education have created negative consequences for Malaysian youth: a mounting [brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital_flight "Human capital flight") in particular. A study from 2000 found that the country had lost an ample amount of talented and skilled youth to other more favourable countries as the result of students from the large non-Malay minority language communities being forced to go to university abroad because they did not attend Malay language schools.[[216]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDefillippiArthurLindsay2009117-217)[[217]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFongZubair201715-218)
