## Culture

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=17 "Edit section: Culture")]

See also: [Culture of Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Malaysia "Culture of Malaysia") and [East Asian cultural sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_cultural_sphere "East Asian cultural sphere")

[![Image 72](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Inside_the_Baba_and_Nyonya_Heritage_Museum.jpg/250px-Inside_the_Baba_and_Nyonya_Heritage_Museum.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Inside_the_Baba_and_Nyonya_Heritage_Museum.jpg)

Inside the former house of a Peranakan (Baba-Nyonya or 峇峇娘惹) in [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca "Malacca") which has now been [converted into a museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baba_Nyonya_Heritage_Museum "Baba Nyonya Heritage Museum"), a Nyonya can be seen sitting inside wearing the traditional _[kebaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebaya#Encim\_or\_Peranakan\_kebaya "Kebaya")_.

[Chinese cultural influences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_culture "Chinese culture") made their mark when [ancient trade relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road "Silk Road") were established with the [Nanyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanyang_(region) "Nanyang (region)") (南洋) region.[[220]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarbie1992-221) The massive ethnic Chinese migration during the British colonial period causing a subsequent strong influence on the country's culture, including in cuisine and the language.[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTENazriShahrimFarahMursyid2017-222) Aside from the Chinese cultural influence on local culture, ethnic Chinese from the first wave migration before English arrival had mainly adopted a localised culture.[[222]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeeChoong200683-223) The Baba-Nyonya in Malacca had a very strong Malay influence in their clothing and food although still maintaining their Chinese heritage of religion, name, and identity.[[223]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Star2008-224) Their food are made with Malay ingredients and recipes but produced using the Chinese method while their houses are constructed with the infusion of [Victorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_architecture "Victorian architecture"), [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_architecture "Chinese architecture") and [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_architecture "Malay architecture") elements.[[223]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Star2008-224) Together with the Peranakans in Penang, both sides use a mixture of [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien "Hokkien") and Malay despite the Penang Peranakans speak mainly Hokkien with some borrowed words from Malay words while in Malacca, the Peranakans have adopted Malay [patois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patois "Patois") with some Hokkien words.[[224]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGovernment_of_Penang-225) The food prepared by Penang Peranakans also infuses both Chinese and Malay elements, albeit with an additional [Thai influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_cuisine "Thai cuisine").[[224]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGovernment_of_Penang-225) The Peranakans in the east coast of Malaysia in Kelantan and Terengganu have much closer ties with the Malay and [Siamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Siamese "Malaysian Siamese") community there since most of them has adopted the Malay and Siamese lifestyle.[[225]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWinzeler197445%E2%80%9361-226)[[226]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTENazriHanapiTeo2011112-227)[[227]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHong2015-228)

[![Image 73](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/NaTukKong003.jpg/250px-NaTukKong003.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NaTukKong003.jpg)

[Na Tuk Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Na_Tuk_Kong "Na Tuk Kong") (拿督公) shrine in West Malaysia.

Further, close blood relations between ethnic Chinese and local indigenous in northern [Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo "Borneo") since the early kingdoms period produced the "Sino-Natives" persons in Sabah as a result of [inter-marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interracial_marriage "Interracial marriage") between ethnic Chinese there and natives of Sabah.[[228]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2018141-229) Instead of speaking Chinese languages, these communities spoke the localized [Sabah Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_Malay "Sabah Malay") dialect as their main _[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua\_franca "Lingua franca")_ since the 14th century.[[228]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2018141-229) The close relations are marked with earliest records of the "Kina" term which is widely used by the indigenous [Dusun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusun_people "Dusun people") to refer to the Chinese ethnicity including for the Dusun homeland mountain of "[Mount Kinabalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Kinabalu "Mount Kinabalu")" (Chinese widow mountain), "[Kinabatangan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinabatangan_River "Kinabatangan River")" (Chinese river) and several other places such as the Kinabalu mountain stream of "Kinataki", a small river in the northeastern coast of "Kinabañgun" and a Chinese land in [Labuan Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labuan "Labuan"), the "Kinabenua".[[229]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTESt._John1863327-230)[[230]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhitehead1893155-231) The [Na Tuk Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Na_Tuk_Kong "Na Tuk Kong") shrine for example shown another sign of assimilation with local culture, where it refers to local Malay guardian spirits worshipped by some ethnic Chinese in West Malaysia and neighbouring [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore "Singapore") and Indonesia.[[231]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELin2017126-232) The term _Na Tuk_ originated from the Malay word of _[Datuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Datuk "Datuk")_ (grandfather) which then merged with _Kong_ (公, sometimes addressed as 'Kung' meaning elderly male), similar to [Tu Ti Kung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudigong "Tudigong") ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language "Chinese language"):[土地公](https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%9C%9F%E5%9C%B0%E5%85%AC "zh:土地公")), an earth deity worshipped in [East Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asia "East Asia").[[232]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHock19968-233)[[233]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELin2017125%E2%80%93126-234)

### Cuisine

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=18 "Edit section: Cuisine")]

Main article: [Malaysian Chinese cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese_cuisine "Malaysian Chinese cuisine")

[![Image 74](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Cuisine_of_Chinese_Malaysian.jpg/250px-Cuisine_of_Chinese_Malaysian.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cuisine_of_Chinese_Malaysian.jpg)

Some of the examples of ethnic Chinese influenced Malaysian cuisine, clockwise from top-right: [grass jelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grass_jelly "Grass jelly") with _[Bandung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandung\_(drink) "Bandung (drink)")_, _[yong tau foo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yong\_tau\_foo "Yong tau foo")_, _[Nyonya steamed layer cake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuih "Kuih")_ and [_laksa_ noodle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laksa "Laksa").

The ethnic Chinese cuisine in Malaysia is derived from the culinary traditions of their early immigrants and descendants, who have adopted or modified their culinary traditions under the influence of Malaysian culture. Additionally due to the immigration of Chinese to the country, [Chinese cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_cuisine "Chinese cuisine") has now become an inseparable part in the country's cultural mixture.[[234]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutton201224-235) When Chinese merchants sailed their [junks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junk_(ship) "Junk (ship)") across the [South China Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China_Sea "South China Sea"), they visited ports in Borneo and Malacca, which had a profound influence on the region.[[234]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutton201224-235) Since the local king at that time enjoyed Chinese cuisine, the traders would bring along cooks with them to the island.[[235]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYuan200019-236) They introduced ingredients in Chinese cooking such as [noodles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noodles "Noodles"), [bean sprouts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bean_sprouts "Bean sprouts"), [tofu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tofu "Tofu") and [soy sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soy_sauce "Soy sauce") which are by now widely used by every ethnic group in the country.[[236]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutton200513-237) Apart from introducing new ingredients, these earlier traders also discovered ingredients among the local population along the coast, such as the expensive [edible bird's nest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edible_bird's_nest "Edible bird's nest") with the best quality nests claiming to come from Borneo.[[237]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChen197642-238)[[238]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunlop1994251-239) Since a vast majority of Chinese Malaysians today are descendants of immigrants from [southern China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China "South China"), local Chinese cuisine is predominantly based from [Fujian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujian_cuisine "Fujian cuisine"), [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese_cuisine "Cantonese cuisine"), [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_cuisine "Hakka cuisine") and [Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_cuisine "Teochew cuisine") cuisines. Their technique of [stir frying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stir_frying "Stir frying") ingredients and adding a small portion of [cooking oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooking_oil "Cooking oil") over high heat in a [wok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wok "Wok") is widely adopted among the nation.[[236]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutton200513-237)

[![Image 75](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Nyonya_kuih_kitchen.jpg/250px-Nyonya_kuih_kitchen.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nyonya_kuih_kitchen.jpg)

The Nyonya making various traditional _[kuih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuih "Kuih")_.

Chinese Malaysian cuisine had developed a strong penchant for [spices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spices "Spices") and [chillies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chillies "Chillies") where any local Chinese [kopi tiam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kopi_tiam "Kopi tiam") or restaurant will offer pickled green or red chillies _[sambal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambal "Sambal")_ for noodles and rice-based meals.[[236]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutton200513-237) They borrowed [curry leaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curry_tree "Curry tree") from the Indians and have since adapted English [Worcestershire sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worcestershire_sauce "Worcestershire sauce") and [tomato sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomato_sauce "Tomato sauce") along with Indian and Malay spices to the [cooking pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooking_pot "Cooking pot").[[236]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutton200513-237) Among the notable Chinese dishes in the country including the _[bak kut teh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bak\_kut\_teh "Bak kut teh")_ (pork rib soup with Chinese herbs), _[char kway teow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Char\_kway\_teow "Char kway teow")_ (stir fried rice noodle), _[dim sum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dim\_sum "Dim sum")_ (ready-to-serve dishes), _[hae mee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hae\_mee "Hae mee")_ (spicy prawn noodle soup served in savoury broth), [Hainanese chicken rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainanese_chicken_rice "Hainanese chicken rice"), _kai si [hor fun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hor\_fun "Hor fun")_ (flat rice noodle soup with chicken slices, shrimps and bean sprouts), _[kolo mee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolo\_mee "Kolo mee")_ (Sarawakian egg noodle served in dark sauce, tomato sauce or chilli sauce), _[lor mee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lor\_mee "Lor mee")_ (thick yellow noodle served in dark sauce and thick broth), _ngiu chap mee_ (special Sabah mixed beef noodle), _[pan mee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan\_mee "Pan mee")_ (handmade noodle soup served with pork balls, minced pork, dried anchovies, [black fungus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood_ear "Wood ear") and vegetable), _[sang nyuk mee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabahan\_cuisine "Sabahan cuisine")_ (special Sabah dry pork noodle or pork noodle soup), _[wonton mee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wonton\_noodle "Wonton noodle")_ (dry noodle or noodle soup served with BBQ pork, minced pork and pork or shrimp dumplings) and _[yong tau foo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yong\_tau\_foo "Yong tau foo")_ (tofu and mixed vegetables filled with ground pork mixture or fish paste). Many Chinese Malaysians also can cook Malay-style chicken or fish with most versions of _[laksa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laksa "Laksa")_ are prepared by them.[[236]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutton200513-237) Some examples of basic Chinese Malaysians drinks that are commercially produced in the country include the [black tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_tea "Black tea"), [chrysanthemum tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysanthemum_tea "Chrysanthemum tea"), [grass jelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grass_jelly "Grass jelly"), [green tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_tea "Green tea"), [jasmine tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasmine_tea "Jasmine tea"), [soy milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soy_milk "Soy milk") and [white coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipoh_white_coffee "Ipoh white coffee") while popular snacks including the _[cakoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youtiao "Youtiao")_ (long deep-fried dough), [egg tart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egg_tart "Egg tart"), _[hum ji peng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ham\_chim\_peng "Ham chim peng")_ (circle shaped fried dough with or without fillings), UFO tart, _[ngo hiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngo\_hiang "Ngo hiang")_ (various spiced, minced meat rolls prepared in deep fried style and served with vegetables), _[pau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baozi "Baozi")_ (steamed buns), _[popiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popiah "Popiah")_ (fried or plain spring rolls filled with mixed vegetables), and _[tau sar piah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tambun\_biscuit "Tambun biscuit")_ (pastry biscuits filled with a sweet or savoury filling).[[239]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan2012137-240) The legacy from the first wave migration created the [Peranakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peranakan "Peranakan") (or Baba-Nyonya) ethnicity through a blend between Chinese and Malay which subsequently produced the [Peranakan cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peranakan_cuisine "Peranakan cuisine") where they served Indian-style curries with eating [etiquette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etiquette "Etiquette") different from mainstream ethnic Chinese society by following the Malay usage of fingers than [chopsticks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chopsticks "Chopsticks").[[240]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest2010486-241) The Baba-Nyonya also specialized in making a variety of local snacks called _[kuih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuih "Kuih")_ which require plenty of patience and skills.[[241]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEArokiasamy2017451-242)

### Dialects and languages

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=19 "Edit section: Dialects and languages")]

See also: [Sinophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinophone "Sinophone") and [Language and overseas Chinese communities §Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_and_overseas_Chinese_communities#Malaysia "Language and overseas Chinese communities")

[![Image 76](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Map-Sinophone_World.png/250px-Map-Sinophone_World.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Map-Sinophone_World.png)

Map of the [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese "Mandarin Chinese")[Sinophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinophone "Sinophone") world, where [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese "Mandarin Chinese") and other [Chinese languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language "Chinese language") are spoken as a [first language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language "First language") among ethnic Chinese in countries where their population is significant:  

Mandarin Chinese-speaking majority (Coastal and central areas of China, [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan "Taiwan") and [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore "Singapore"))

Large Mandarin Chinese-speaking minority (Highland China, [Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia "Malaysia") and inner [Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar "Myanmar"))

Small Mandarin Chinese-speaking minority ([Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei "Brunei"))

**Example of Chinese [loanwords in Malay language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_loanwords_in_Malay "List of loanwords in Malay")**| Loanword | English description |
| :---: | :---: |
| _\_bihun/mihun\__ | [rice vermicelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_vermicelli "Rice vermicelli") |
| _\_cakoi\__ | [a long deep-fried dough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youtiao "Youtiao") |
| _\_cawan\__ | [cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cup "Cup") |
| _\_cincai\__ | carelessly, in a hurry, random |
| _\_cincau\__ | [grass jelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grass_jelly "Grass jelly") |
| _\_ginseng\__ | [root of herbal plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginseng "Ginseng") |
| _\_kapcai\__ | [underbone motorcycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underbone "Underbone") |
| _\_kuaci\__ | [sunflower seed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunflower_seed "Sunflower seed") |
| _\_kuetiau\__ | [rice noodle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Char_kway_teow "Char kway teow") |
| _\_kuih\__ | [snack and dessert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuih "Kuih") |
| _\_longkang\__ | [drainage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage "Drainage") |
| _\_mee/mi\__ | [noodle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noodle "Noodle") |
| _\_pau\__ | [steamed bun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baozi "Baozi") |
| _\_popia/popiah\__ | [fried spring roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popiah "Popiah") |
| _\_samseng\__ | [gangster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangster "Gangster") |
| _\_tauhu\__ | [tofu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tofu "Tofu") |
| _\_tauke\__ | [big boss](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/towkay "wikt:towkay") |
| _\_teh\__ | [tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea "Tea") |
| _\_tongkang\__ | [Chinese sailing ship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junk_(ship) "Junk (ship)") |
| Source: [Asmah 1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#CITEREFAsmah1975), p.40 and [DBP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dewan_Bahasa_dan_Pustaka "Dewan Bahasa dan Pustaka") |

The 1970 Malaysian Census reported [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien "Hokkien") as the largest Chinese subgroup, accounting for 34.2% of the country's Chinese population, followed by [Hakka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakka_Chinese "Hakka Chinese") with 22.1%, [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese "Cantonese") with 19.8%, [Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_Min "Teochew Min") with 12.4% and [Hainanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainanese "Hainanese") with 4.7%.[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang201212-173) In 2003, more detailed statistics for each language reported Hokkien with 2,020,914, Hakka with 1,092,835, Cantonese with 1,068,008, Teochew with 497,280, [Hokchew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_dialect "Fuzhou dialect") with 251,553, Hainanese with 141,045, Kwongsai with 51,674, [Henghua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pu-Xian_Min "Pu-Xian Min") with 24,654, Hockchia with 14,935, and different other ethnic Chinese with 202,977, for a total of 5,365,875.[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004698-149) The Malaysian Hokkien are divided into two localised dialects; the [Penang Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penang_Hokkien "Penang Hokkien") (northern) comprising [Penang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penang "Penang"), [Kedah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedah "Kedah"), [Perlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perlis "Perlis") and [Perak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perak "Perak"), and [Southern Peninsular Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Peninsular_Malaysian_Hokkien "Southern Peninsular Malaysian Hokkien") in [Johor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor "Johor"), [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca "Malacca") and neighbouring Singapore.[[242]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEArmstrongArmstrongMulliner2012218-243) The northern Hokkien contains more Malay loanwords than the southern with the latter maintaining more of their original Hokkien words.[[242]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEArmstrongArmstrongMulliner2012218-243) Many Hokkien living in Sarawak have been influenced heavily by indigenous language similarly with Hokkien in Kelantan where their language was heavily localised following close interaction with local Malays and [Siamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Siamese "Malaysian Siamese").[[242]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEArmstrongArmstrongMulliner2012218-243) Localised Hokkien also spoken primarily by the Peranakan community (Baba-Nyonya) in both Malacca and Penang.[[243]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheungTan2007171-244) Generally, Hokkien became a contact language among ethnic Chinese of different linguistic background in most parts of Malaysia.[[244]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWurmM%C3%BChlh%C3%A4uslerTryon2011698-245)

The [Malaysian Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Cantonese "Malaysian Cantonese") became the contact language in the Malaysia's capital of Kuala Lumpur albeit with a distinct [phonology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phonology "Phonology") and grammar from standard Cantonese spoken in [Pearl River Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl_River_Delta "Pearl River Delta") region of China including in [Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong "Hong Kong") and [Macau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macau "Macau").[[244]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWurmM%C3%BChlh%C3%A4uslerTryon2011698-245)[[245]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsmah200451-246)[[246]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauerBenedict2011281-247) Hakka assumes the role of contact language in Sabah but in the rest of the country the language is more commonly used as an intra-group language than a [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca "Lingua franca") within the Chinese community, with about 66% of Hakka in Johor preferring [localised Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Mandarin "Malaysian Mandarin").[[247]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang201280-248)[[248]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlattWeberHo198350-249) Similar studies on [Eastern Min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Min "Eastern Min") speakers of the Fuzhou community in Sarawak also found a general shift of language choice from their own language to [Mandarin Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese "Mandarin Chinese") and English.[[249]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2009240-250) Despite the Teochew not being the largest subgroup, Teochew has become a bridge language for trade between Teochew exporters in China and wholesalers in Malaysia.[[250]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaCartier200385-251)[[251]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton2011124-252) Hainanese is another minority language, within the [Min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Min_Chinese "Min Chinese") branch.[[252]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang201218-253) These languages, and others like Henghua, are also often called "dialects", despite being linguistically distinct [Chinese languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Chinese "Varieties of Chinese").[[253]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsmah199226-254) Mandarin, originally spoken by a small northern Chinese minority, is becoming a major [working language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_language "Working language") among all Chinese in the country, with [Standard Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Mandarin "Standard Mandarin") used as the [medium of instruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medium_of_instruction "Medium of instruction") in local Chinese-medium schools and [independent high school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_independent_high_school "Chinese independent high school"), although the language is still not necessarily spoken in their daily life.[[254]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-255) Based on early Malaysian Census in 1970, around 41% of ethnic Chinese Malaysians are able to converse in Mandarin.[[255]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang201216%E2%80%9317-256)

### Holidays and festivities

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=20 "Edit section: Holidays and festivities")]

See also: [Public holidays in Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Malaysia "Public holidays in Malaysia")

[![Image 77](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Lion_dance_in_Kuala_Lumpur%2C_Malaysia.jpg/250px-Lion_dance_in_Kuala_Lumpur%2C_Malaysia.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lion_dance_in_Kuala_Lumpur,_Malaysia.jpg)

[Lion dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lion_dance "Lion dance") in Malaysia's capital during the Chinese main festivity.

With the large presence of ethnic Chinese in the country nationwide, [Chinese New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_New_Year "Chinese New Year") is celebrated as a national public holiday. [13 states and three federal territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_and_federal_territories_of_Malaysia "States and federal territories of Malaysia") in the country celebrate with two days holidays.[[256]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGovernment_of_Malaysia2019-257) During the New Year, many Chinese living with their family in the urban areas will celebrate together, while some who may have family in rural areas will return for family reunions. Traditional dinner with the entire family is the most important aspect of the celebration and may include a traditional food tossing of _[yee sang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusheng "Yusheng"),_ especially among the Cantonese.[[257]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeong19922-258)[[258]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDK_Travel201634-259) Elders will usually give the young a [red envelope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_envelope "Red envelope") (_[ang pow](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/ang%20pow "wikt:ang pow")_ or _angpau_). A [variety of festival sweets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_candy_box "Chinese candy box") are presented in the house for visitors. Most Chinese settlements and [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown "Chinatown") streets will be decorated before the New Year with colorful lanterns. [Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_dance "Dragon dance") and [lion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lion_dance "Lion dance") dances will be performed with [firecracker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firecracker "Firecracker") and [fireworks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fireworks "Fireworks") shows also featured as part of the celebrations.[[258]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDK_Travel201634-259)

[![Image 78](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Chinese_New_Year_foods_in_Malaysia.jpg/250px-Chinese_New_Year_foods_in_Malaysia.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Chinese_New_Year_foods_in_Malaysia.jpg)

Aside from [mandarin orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_orange "Mandarin orange"), various other snacks are presented for visitors throughout the Chinese New Year.

[Chingay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chingay_parade "Chingay parade") processions are also being held as part of the New Year festivities especially in Johor Bahru and Penang.[[259]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFodorFisher1986297-260) Several other festivals are celebrated through the seasons in a year including the [Dongzhi Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dongzhi_Festival "Dongzhi Festival"), [Dragon Boat Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_Boat_Festival "Dragon Boat Festival"), [Hungry Ghost Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungry_Ghost_Festival "Hungry Ghost Festival"), [Lantern Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lantern_Festival "Lantern Festival") (_Chap Goh Mei_), [Mid-Autumn Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-Autumn_Festival "Mid-Autumn Festival"), [Nine Emperor Gods Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nine_Emperor_Gods_Festival "Nine Emperor Gods Festival") and [Qingming Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qingming_Festival "Qingming Festival") which originated from much of [Chinese folk beliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folklore "Chinese folklore") and traditional agricultural society.[[260]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKohHo2009132-261) Throughout the festivals, especially for major Chinese festivals such as the New Year, Hungry Ghost, and Mid-Autumn, Chinese temples will organize cultural and religious activities.[[261]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan2018103-262) The Hungry Ghost Festival is generally not observed by most _[Dejiao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De\_teaching "De teaching")_ (_Zi_) group in Malaysia which descended directly from _Dejiao_ association in China, it is only mainly observed by three _Zi_ associations in Kuala Lumpur (_Zi Fang Ge_), [Pulau Ketam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulau_Ketam "Pulau Ketam") (_Zi Bang Ge_) and [Butterworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butterworth,_Penang "Butterworth, Penang") (_Zi Wei Gi_) with the three calling the festival as _Wanyuan Shenghui_ than _Yulan Shanghui_.[[262]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan198572-263) The Mooncake Festival is celebrated through the Mid-Autumn Festival where there people will exchange and eat [mooncake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mooncake "Mooncake").[[263]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDK_Travel201659%E2%80%9360-264) Traditional [Chinese weddings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_marriage "Chinese marriage") remain popular to some ethnic Chinese Malaysian couples even though most have preferred the Western-influenced wearing of white [wedding dresses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wedding_dress "Wedding dress") and black [jackets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_tie "Black tie").[[264]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTELim2014-265)

### Religion

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malaysian_Chinese&action=edit&section=21 "Edit section: Religion")]

See also: [Chinese folk religion in Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religion_in_Southeast_Asia "Chinese folk religion in Southeast Asia"), [Buddhism in Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Malaysia "Buddhism in Malaysia"), [Taoism in Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism_in_Malaysia "Taoism in Malaysia"), and [Malaysian folk religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_folk_religion "Malaysian folk religion")

[![Image 79: Kek Lok Si](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/MY-penang-george-kek-lok-si-tempel-garten-1.jpg/250px-MY-penang-george-kek-lok-si-tempel-garten-1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kek_Lok_Si "Kek Lok Si")

[![Image 80: Jade Dragon Temple](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Tian_Wang_Dian.jpg/250px-Tian_Wang_Dian.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jade_Dragon_Temple "Jade Dragon Temple")

[![Image 81: Tam Kung Temple](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Sandakan_Sabah_Tam-Kong-Temple-01.jpg/250px-Sandakan_Sabah_Tam-Kong-Temple-01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tam_Kung_Temple,_Malaysia "Tam Kung Temple, Malaysia")

[![Image 82: Sam Poh Tong Temple](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/Sam_Poh_Tong_Temple.jpg/250px-Sam_Poh_Tong_Temple.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Poh_Tong_Temple "Sam Poh Tong Temple")

[Chinese folk religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religion "Chinese folk religion"), [Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism "Confucianism"), [Mahāyāna Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81y%C4%81na_Buddhism "Mahāyāna Buddhism") and [Taoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism "Taoism") temples in Malaysia.

Religion of ethnic Chinese Malaysians (2010)[[265]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDepartment_of_Statistics_Malaysia2010b15-266)

1.   [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism") (83.6%)
2.   [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity "Christianity") (11.1%)
3.   [Folk beliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religion "Chinese folk religion") (3.40%)
4.   [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam") (0.70%)
5.   [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion "Irreligion") (0.80%)
6.   [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism") (0.20%)
7.   Other religion (0.13%)
8.   Unknown (0.17%)

Based on the 2000 Malaysian Census, 86.6% of ethnic Chinese Malaysians adhere to [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism") and [Chinese folk religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religion "Chinese folk religion") with numerous [Chinese temples and shrines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_temple "Chinese temple") visible nationwide.[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004703-267) More detailed statistics in 2010 found a total of 5,341,687 ethnic Chinese Malaysians adhere to Buddhism, 695,479 are [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity "Christianity"), 218,261 practising [Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism "Confucianism"), [Taoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism "Taoism") and other [Chinese folk religious sects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religious_sects "Chinese folk religious sects"), 60,320 are [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam"), 42,048 are [irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion "Irreligion"), 14,878 are [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism"), 11,387 with unknown religion while 8,576 practising other different religion.[[265]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDepartment_of_Statistics_Malaysia2010b15-266) Among the common [deities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shen_(Chinese_folk_religion) "Shen (Chinese folk religion)") worshipped by ethnic Chinese Malaysians in temples are [Guan Yu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guan_Yu#Worship "Guan Yu"), [Guanyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanyin "Guanyin"), [Tua Pek Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tua_Pek_Kong "Tua Pek Kong") and [Mazu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazu "Mazu").[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004703-267)

Commonly found in ethnic Chinese homes and shops are [altars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altars "Altars") installed for deities of their affinities together with [ancestral worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_ancestral_worship "Chinese ancestral worship"). Most of the deities are of Chinese origin but it is still common to see a few local deities.[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004703-267) Most, if not all cities in Malaysia include the presence of Chinese Buddhist temples and societies. However, there exists towns with only Buddhist statues, although some may have one or two other Chinese deities.[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004703-267) Other [Buddhist branches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_of_Buddhism "Schools of Buddhism") of [Theravāda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Therav%C4%81da "Theravāda") and various [Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhism "Tibetan Buddhism") traditions ([Vajrayāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajray%C4%81na "Vajrayāna")) also have Chinese members and some of the buildings of these branches were built with the funds and [philanthropic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philanthropic "Philanthropic") efforts from local Chinese Buddhists, examples being the establishment of [Buddhist Maha Vihara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_Maha_Vihara,_Brickfields "Buddhist Maha Vihara, Brickfields") in Kuala Lumpur and [Wat Chayamangkalaram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat_Chayamangkalaram "Wat Chayamangkalaram") in Penang.[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004703-267)[[267]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhoo2012313-268)[[268]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan201835-269) Unlike the [institutional religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_religion "Institutional religion") of Buddhism, Christianity and Islam, ethnic Chinese who follow traditional folk religion do not have separate names for their belief and practices, as similar to the indigenous Malaysians such as [Iban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iban_people "Iban people") and the [Orang Asli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orang_Asli "Orang Asli").[[269]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan_Lee_Ooi20188-270) They describe their religious beliefs as either _bai shen_ or _bai fo_ (worshipping deities) which does usually include the worshipping of Buddha since Mahayana Buddhist deities are also commonly worshipped by the followers of Chinese traditional religion.[[269]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan_Lee_Ooi20188-270)

[![Image 83](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/Palm_Sunday_at_St_Ignatius_Church_in_Petaling_Jaya%2C_Selangor.jpg/250px-Palm_Sunday_at_St_Ignatius_Church_in_Petaling_Jaya%2C_Selangor.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Palm_Sunday_at_St_Ignatius_Church_in_Petaling_Jaya,_Selangor.jpg)

Malaysian Chinese [Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Malaysia "Catholic Church in Malaysia") during [Palm Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_Sunday "Palm Sunday") at St Ignatius Church in [Selangor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selangor "Selangor").

Chinese Christians, including both [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church") and [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism "Protestantism") presence, are mainly visible with their active missionary activities especially among ethnic Chinese in East Malaysia, having a large proportion compared to other regions.[[270]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest2010485%E2%80%93486-271) The majority of Christians in West Malaysia also are of Chinese origin.[[271]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoh200555-272) Most early Chinese churches including [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists "Baptists"), [Methodists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodists "Methodists") and [Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterians "Presbyterians") have their origins from missionary migrants that introduced the missions established in China to Malaysia.[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004703-267) The number of Chinese Muslims is very small compared to Chinese Christians since the latter religion is seen as more tolerant, mainly due to the general perception that embracing [Islam in Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Malaysia "Islam in Malaysia") is to become [Malayised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayisation "Malayisation"), in addition to the ethnic rivalry between Malay and Chinese that makes Islam as less desirable to ethnic Chinese Malaysians.[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004703-267) A majority of Chinese Malaysians, especially those who have received Chinese education, follow Confucian values and to a lesser extent, Taoism. Some that are more traditional may hold more conservative views on [filial piety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filial_piety "Filial piety") and [social relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_relation "Social relation"), while also consulting [Chinese horoscopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_horoscope "Chinese horoscope") as well on Chinese [geomancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geomancy "Geomancy") of _[feng shui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feng\_shui "Feng shui")_.[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmberEmberSkoggard2004703-267) Taoist activities gain increasing interests and significance among Chinese Malaysians although it is not deeply practiced.[[272]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPas199836-273)[[273]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Chinese#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhoo20142-274)
